No production runtime source was modified. The dirty `decode.rs` / `decode_session.rs`
in the tree are the prior long-context KV fix (`sebastian-long-context`), not this task.


<!-- merged from .squad/decisions/inbox/sebastian-long-context.md -->
# Decision: fp32 GQA CPU models take the O(1)/token shared-buffer KV path

**Date:** 2026-07-12
**Author:** Sebastian (Performance)
**Area:** runtime KV decode path selection (`onnx-genai-engine`)
**Status:** implemented + measured (not committed — coordinator commits)

## Problem

Per the definitive CPU benchmark
(`docs/benchmarks/2026-07-12-JustindeMacBook-Pro-cpu-recipe.md`), onnx-genai
decode degrades badly with context length while llama.cpp/ORT-Foundry stay
flat:

| | short (59-tok prompt) | long (858-tok prompt) |
|---|---|---|
| onnx-genai | 158.6 | **115.2** |
| LM Studio  | 155.6 | **160.2** |
| Foundry    | 202.7 | 165.8 |

We win short, lose long. Our per-token cost scales with context; theirs does not.

## Root cause (with profiler evidence)

The CPU-recipe model (`models/qwen2.5-0.5b-cpu-recipe`, Mobius `build-gguf
--ep cpu`) is a **GroupQueryAttention** model with a **growing** KV contract:

- inputs `past_key_values.N.{key,value}` = `[batch, 2, past_sequence_len, 64]`
- outputs `present.N.{key,value}`        = `[batch, 2, past_sequence_len + sequence_len, 64]`
- fp32 KV, 24 GQA nodes, no static-cache signature, no
  `past_present_share_buffer` ORT metadata.

Our runtime selects the KV binding strategy in
`onnx-genai-engine::decode::shared_kv_buffer_len_from_metadata`. That gate only
returned a shared-buffer max length for **GQA + fp16** KV (added for the WebGPU
fp16 export). This model is **GQA + fp32**, so it fell through to
`DecodeKvMode::ZeroCopyRebind` — the **growing** KV path: every decode step ORT
allocates a fresh `present` of size `past+1` and concatenates the entire past
into it, then that value is rebound as the next step's `past`. That is
**O(context) memory traffic per token**, so per-token cost grows with context.

Decode-only profiler evidence (`long_context_bench`, same DecodeSession the
engine uses; Apple M1 Max, default threads):

| context depth | growing KV (ZeroCopyRebind) | shared-buffer |
|---|---|---|
| 1–64      | 5.33 ms/tok (187.6 tok/s) | 4.43 ms/tok (226 tok/s) |
| 859–922*  | **8.94 ms/tok (111.9 tok/s)** | **6.33 ms/tok (158.1 tok/s)** |

\* matches the benchmark's long case (858-tok prompt + decode). RSS also grows
under the growing path (312→360 MB) but stays flat under shared-buffer, direct
evidence the KV buffer is reallocated/grown each step.

The growing-path 111.9 tok/s reproduces the reported 115.2 tok/s. Mechanism
named: **growing KV cache (no in-place/share-buffer) causing O(context)/token
KV concatenation + reallocation each step.**

## Whose issue: OURS (runtime path selection)

Not a model-build defect. The GQA graph already supports in-place share-buffer
KV; the runtime's `DecodeSession` `SharedBuffer` machinery already exists and
already accepts fp32 KV. The only thing wrong was the **fp16-only gate** that
excluded fp32 GQA models from that path.

## Fix

`crates/onnx-genai-engine/src/decode.rs`: broaden the share-buffer dtype gate
from fp16-only to **fp16 or fp32** (the ORT GQA kernel supports
`past_present_share_buffer` for both):

- `metadata_kv_is_fp16` → `metadata_kv_is_share_buffer_dtype`
- `is_fp16_dtype` → `is_share_buffer_kv_dtype` (adds `float32`/`fp32`/`float`)
- updated docstrings on `shared_kv_buffer_len_from_metadata` and
  `detect_model_decode_path`.

With the fix the engine routes the CPU-recipe model to
`PastPresent { shared_buffer: true, max_len: Some(4096) }` (verified at load
time). Non-GQA, unsupported-dtype (int8/bf16), static-cache, and sliding-window
paths are unchanged.

**Correctness:** the greedy token trace is **bit-identical** between the growing
and shared-buffer paths (24-token trace and final token match exactly), and
generated text stays coherent.

## Result

Long-context decode: **111.9 → 158.1 tok/s (+41%)**, closing the gap to LM
Studio (160.2) and near Foundry (165.8). Short context is also slightly faster
(no per-step concat allocation); no regression.

## Files changed (onnx-genai repo only; NOT committed)

- `crates/onnx-genai-engine/src/decode.rs` — broaden share-buffer gate to fp32
  GQA; update helpers, docstrings, and unit tests
  (`shared_kv_from_gqa_fp32_native_dtype`, `no_shared_kv_when_unsupported_kv_dtype`,
  `recognizes_share_buffer_kv_dtype_variants`).
- `crates/onnx-genai-ort/examples/long_context_bench.rs` — add `--mode shared`
  diagnostic and per-run token trace for correctness comparison.
- `docs/benchmarks/2026-07-12-JustindeMacBook-Pro-cpu-recipe.md` — record the
  long-context root cause and the shared-buffer fix result.

## Validation

- `cargo test -p onnx-genai-engine -p onnx-genai-ort` — green.
- `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings` — clean.
- Coherent output + bit-identical greedy trace verified on the CPU-recipe model.

## Handoff / follow-ups

- No Mobius change required for this fix. Optional Mobius nicety (Sapper): also
  emit an explicit `past_present_share_buffer` custom-metadata flag on CPU GQA
  exports so the runtime does not depend solely on inference-metadata dtype
  inference.
- Remaining CPU decode gap vs Foundry is now prefill/TTFT and the 169-vs-121
  MatMul packing (fuse Q/K/V), tracked separately.


<!-- merged from .squad/decisions/inbox/sebastian-m1max-roofline.md -->
# Decision: Parameterized Apple Silicon CPU Roofline for LLM Decode

**By:** Sebastian (Performance Engineer)
**Date:** 2026-07-26 (updated 2026-07-27 per portability constraint)
**Branch:** `squad/mac-cpu-ep-roofline` (PR #227)
**Constraint:** [coordinator-apple-silicon-portability.md] — optimizations must be general across the full M1–M4 family; M1 Max is the measurement rig, not the target.

---

## 1. Parameterized Roofline Model

### The Formula

For any FP32 model at batch=1 decode:

```
ceiling_toks = BW_cpu / W_bytes
```

Where:
- `BW_cpu` = sustained CPU memory read bandwidth (GB/s), **measured at startup**
- `W_bytes` = model weight bytes read per token (GB)
- `ceiling_toks` = bandwidth-bound decode throughput (tok/s)

Arithmetic intensity for batch-1 decode: `AI = 2 / bytes_per_param`. For FP32: `AI = 2/4 = 0.50 FLOP/byte`. This is deep in the bandwidth-bound regime on every Apple Silicon part (crossover to compute-bound requires AI > BW_cpu / GFLOPS_peak, which is ~1–4 FLOP/byte depending on chip — well above 0.50).

### How the Ceiling Moves Across Apple Silicon

Apple Silicon advertised memory bandwidth is shared with GPU. CPU-achievable bandwidth is a fraction of it. On this M1 Max, I measured **197 GB/s from 8 P-cores** against 400 GB/s advertised (49%). The exact ratio varies by chip generation and memory controller design, so it **must be measured, not looked up**.

Projections for Qwen2.5-0.5B FP32 (W = 1.932 GB), using known advertised BW and an estimated 40–55% CPU-achievable fraction:

| Chip | Advertised BW | Est. CPU BW (P-cores) | Est. Ceiling tok/s | P-cores |
|------|--------------|----------------------|-------------------|---------|
| M1 (Air/base) | 68 GB/s | 27–37 GB/s | 14–19 | 4 |
| M1 Pro | 200 GB/s | 80–110 GB/s | 41–57 | 6–8 |
| **M1 Max** | 400 GB/s | **197 GB/s (measured)** | **102** | 8 |
| M2 (Air/base) | 100 GB/s | 40–55 GB/s | 21–28 | 4 |
| M3 Pro | 150 GB/s | 60–82 GB/s | 31–42 | 5–6 |
| M4 Pro | 273 GB/s | 109–150 GB/s | 56–78 | 10 |
| M4 Max | 546 GB/s | 218–300 GB/s | 113–155 | 12–16 |

**Key insight: "decode is bandwidth-bound" holds universally, but the ceiling varies 10×.** On an M1 Air, the FP32 ceiling may be as low as 14 tok/s — ORT's 45 tok/s is physically unreachable there at FP32 (ORT itself would be proportionally slower on that hardware). On an M4 Max, the ceiling could be 150+ tok/s.

### ORT's Position Is Chip-Relative

ORT's 45.45 tok/s on this M1 Max = 88 GB/s / 197 GB/s = **44.6% of this machine's ceiling**. ORT uses MLAS with multi-threaded GEMV internally, and that ~45% utilization is likely to be similar (within ±15%) across Apple Silicon because MLAS's threading scales with core count. The question "can we beat ORT?" is therefore about achieving >45% of whatever BW the specific chip offers — the answer is the same everywhere: improve kernel threading and vectorization quality.

---

## 2. Measured Ceilings (M1 Max — one worked instance)

All numbers from micro-benchmarks executed on this exact hardware. These are DATA POINTS in the parameterized model, not universal constants.

### Memory Bandwidth (sequential read, 256 MiB/thread, from DRAM)

| Threads | GB/s | Query at runtime |
|---------|------|------------------|
| 1 P-core | 60.2 | — |
| 2 P-cores | 109.8 | — |
| 4 P-cores | 134.8 | — |
| 8 P-cores | 196.8 | `hw.perflevel0.physicalcpu` = 8 |
| 2 E-cores | 118.1 | `hw.perflevel1.physicalcpu` = 2 |
| 8P + 2E | 259.3 | `hw.ncpu` = 10 |

Scaling is sub-linear: BW ≈ 60 × N^0.58 for N P-cores on this chip. But this exponent is NOT portable — it depends on memory controller width and SLC partitioning. Must be measured per machine.

### Cache Hierarchy (single P-core, this machine)

| Level | Size (queried via sysctl) | Read BW |
|-------|---------------------------|---------|
| L1D | 128 KB (`hw.perflevel0.l1dcachesize`) | 66 GB/s |
| L2 | 12 MB / 4-core cluster (`hw.perflevel0.l2cachesize`) | 60–67 GB/s |
| SLC | 48 MB (not queryable — must probe) | 46–54 GB/s |
| DRAM | — | 46–50 GB/s |

### FP32 Compute Throughput

| Configuration | GFLOPS |
|---------------|--------|
| 1 P-core (NEON FMA) | 46.1 |
| 4 P-cores (NEON FMA) | 154.4 |
| 8 P-cores (NEON FMA) | 139.3 (thermal throttle at 8) |
| Accelerate AMX (M=16, 896²) | 1,351 |
| Accelerate AMX (M=512, 896²) | 2,449 |

AMX is only reachable through Accelerate. NEON FMA alone peaks at ~46 GFLOPS/core — 50× less than AMX. This matters for prefill (compute-bound) but not for decode (bandwidth-bound).

### Achievable GEMV Bandwidth (MT NEON, row-parallel, 4864×896)

| Threads | GB/s |
|---------|------|
| 1 | 47.2 |
| 4 | 82.4 |
| 8 | 112.0 |

### Accelerate cblas_sgemv (internally multi-threaded)

| Matrix | Effective BW | Note |
|--------|-------------|------|
| 896×896 (3.2 MB, fits L2) | 149.5 GB/s | Excellent — cache-resident |
| 4864×896 (17.4 MB, exceeds L2) | 44.9 GB/s | DRAM-limited, poor scaling |

### Simulated Full Decode Step (Accelerate GEMV, all 24 layers)

Total linear-layer time: 28.2 ms → 35.4 tok/s, effective 50.8 GB/s.
Accelerate's internal GEMV threading underperforms for large matrices.

### Multi-threaded NEON GEMV Full Decode (8 threads, all layers)

Total linear-layer time: 28.15 ms → 35.5 tok/s, effective 50.8 GB/s.
Thread-spawn overhead on small matrices (kv_proj: 128×896) killed aggregate BW. With persistent pool this improves substantially.

---

## 3. Where ORT and Native Sit (this M1 Max)

| Backend | tok/s | Eff. BW | % of this machine's ceiling |
|---------|-------|---------|----------------------------|
| ORT | 45.45 | 88 GB/s | 44.6% |
| Native | 3.48 | 5.8 GB/s | 2.9% |
| **Gap** | **13×** | **15×** | — |

### Prefill Regime (separate, compute-bound)

For prefill (40 tokens): AI = 20 FLOP/byte → compute-bound.
- Accelerate AMX: 32.7 ms for linear layers (874 GFLOPS effective)
- ORT: 120 ms TTFT
- Native: 2396 ms TTFT
- AMX peak on this chip: ~2.4 TFLOPS

---

## 4. Root Cause (universal across ALL Apple Silicon)

**The native CPU EP falls through to `gemm_generic` on macOS.** This is not an M1 Max problem — it affects every Mac.

Code path (`src/backend.rs:73–86`, `src/kernels/matmul.rs:146–173`):
1. `CpuBackend::auto_detect()` returns `Accelerate` on macOS ✓
2. But `gemm_with_backend()` matches `Accelerate` via `_ =>` wildcard → calls `gemm_generic` ✗
3. Comment says: *"Accelerate placeholder — currently routes to Generic arithmetic"*

`gemm_generic` pathology for decode (M=1):
- Parallelizes over M output rows via `c.par_chunks_mut(mc * n)`
- **M=1 → exactly 1 row → zero parallelism** (every Apple Silicon chip, every model)
- 4×4 register tile with no explicit NEON intrinsics
- Relies on compiler auto-vectorization (partial at best)
- Result: effectively single-threaded, ~6 GB/s on this machine

**This is the same bug on every Mac.** An M1 Air with 27 GB/s of available bandwidth would see ~6 GB/s achieved = 22% utilization (slightly less pathological in ratio, but still catastrophically slow in absolute terms).

---

## 5. The Decisive Answer

**This is a UNIVERSAL kernel-quality + threading problem.** It is NOT chip-specific.

The 13× gap between ORT and native exists because the native EP runs single-threaded scalar GEMV on every Mac. The fix is the same everywhere: multi-threaded vectorized GEMV through a code path that scales with the hardware.

Whether the fix "beats ORT" depends on the chip's bandwidth tier:
- On high-BW parts (Pro/Max/Ultra): headroom above ORT exists — a well-threaded GEMV can achieve >45% BW utilization
- On low-BW parts (base M1/M2): ORT and native will converge toward the same BW ceiling — the goal is to *match* ORT, not necessarily beat it
- The relative improvement (native going from 3% → 40–60% of ceiling) is universal

---

## 6. Ranked Fixes — Universal vs Tier-Dependent

### 🟢 UNIVERSAL: helps every Apple Silicon part

#### Fix 1: Wire Accelerate BLAS in the `Accelerate` backend match arm
- **What:** Replace `gemm_generic` fallback with `cblas_sgemv` (M=1) and `cblas_sgemm` (M>1)
- **Why universal:** Accelerate ships on every macOS. It internally detects the chip, picks optimal thread count, and routes to AMX where available. No hardcoded constants needed.
- **Expected on M1 Max:** ~35 tok/s decode (measured), 120 ms TTFT (matches ORT)
- **Expected on M1 Air:** ~14 tok/s (near that machine's ceiling)
- **Code change:** One match arm in `gemm_with_backend`, ~20 lines + link `-framework Accelerate`
- **Why it doesn't beat ORT alone:** Accelerate's GEMV is suboptimal for large matrices (44.9 GB/s vs 88 GB/s ORT on this machine). But it immediately closes the 13× gap to <1.3×.

#### Fix 2: Row-parallel NEON GEMV on the existing Rayon/SPMD pool
- **What:** For the M=1 decode path, partition the N (output) dimension across available worker threads. Each thread computes its rows using `float32x4_t` FMA (4-way unrolled inner K-reduction). Use the existing persistent Rayon or SPMD decode pool — NOT `pthread_create` per call.
- **Why universal:** NEON `vfmaq_f32` is baseline ARMv8.0 — available on every Apple Silicon and every ARM64 chip. Thread count derived at runtime from `hw.perflevel0.physicalcpu` (or `rayon::current_num_threads()`). No hardcoded constants.
- **Measured on M1 Max:** 112 GB/s achieved (8 threads, large matrices) → 58 tok/s
- **Projected on M1 Air:** 4 P-cores × ~single-core scaling → ~30 GB/s → 15 tok/s (near ceiling)
- **Why this can beat ORT:** On Pro/Max/Ultra parts, custom GEMV with persistent threads avoids Accelerate's GEMV overhead for large matrices. On base parts, it matches ORT at the shared BW ceiling.

#### Fix 3: Eliminate the single-threaded M=1 bottleneck
- **What:** If neither Fix 1 nor Fix 2, at minimum ensure `gemm_generic` parallelizes over N (columns) not M (rows) when M < thread_count. This is a 3-line change to the existing code.
- **Why universal:** The current `par_chunks_mut(mc * n)` splits on M. When M=1, no parallelism is possible regardless of core count. Splitting on N fixes this everywhere.

### 🟡 TIER-DEPENDENT: payoff varies by chip bandwidth/core count

#### Fix 4: GEMV tile size derived from runtime-queried L2 cache
- **What:** Block the K dimension of GEMV so that each thread's weight tile fits in L2. Query `hw.perflevel0.l2cachesize` and `hw.perflevel0.cpusperl2` at startup; set `KC = (l2_size / cpus_per_l2) / (sizeof(f32) * N_tile)`.
- **Tier dependency:** L2 varies from 4 MB (shared by 4 on M1 base) to 48 MB (M4 Max). A fixed KC=256 (from `gemm_generic`) may be fine on one chip but miss on another.
- **Impact:** 5–15% improvement on chips where the default KC is misaligned with L2. Zero impact where it happens to be right.

#### Fix 5: INT4 / INT8 quantization
- **What:** Reduce weight bytes per token by 4–8×.
- **Tier dependency:** On high-BW parts (Max/Ultra), this moves the ceiling from ~100 to ~400+ tok/s (massive win). On base M1 (27 GB/s), it moves the ceiling from ~14 to ~56 tok/s (still a big win, but the absolute headroom is smaller and compute starts to matter).
- **Universal validity:** AI increases to 2–4 FLOP/byte with INT4. On low-BW parts this stays BW-bound. On Ultra (800 GB/s) with INT4, the compute ceiling (~46 GFLOPS/core × 16 cores = 736 GFLOPS → 736 / (2 × 0.125B params) ≈ 2900 tok/s) is still above the BW ceiling (800 / 0.24 = 3333 tok/s), so still BW-bound.
- **Requires:** `MatMulNBits` kernel quality, which is a separate workstream.

#### Fix 6: Prefill via Accelerate GEMM (AMX)
- **What:** Wire `cblas_sgemm` for the M>1 path to reach the chip's matrix coprocessor.
- **Tier dependency:** AMX throughput varies by generation. M1 AMX ≈ 2 TFLOPS FP32, M4 AMX may differ. Accelerate abstracts this — but the absolute TTFT improvement is larger on chips with more AMX throughput.
- **Universal validity:** Accelerate is universal; the EP just needs to call it.

---

## 7. Runtime Detection Recipe

The EP should query these at initialization (all available via `sysctl`):

```rust
// Core topology
let p_cores: u32 = sysctl("hw.perflevel0.physicalcpu");  // 4–16
let e_cores: u32 = sysctl("hw.perflevel1.physicalcpu");  // 2–8
let total_cores: u32 = sysctl("hw.ncpu");                // 8–24

// Cache sizes (for tile blocking)
let l1d: usize = sysctl("hw.perflevel0.l1dcachesize");   // 128 KB (M1–M4)
let l2: usize  = sysctl("hw.perflevel0.l2cachesize");    // 4–48 MB
let cpus_per_l2: u32 = sysctl("hw.perflevel0.cpusperl2");// 2–4

// Feature detection
let has_fp16: bool = sysctl("hw.optional.arm.FEAT_FP16"); // all Apple Silicon = true
let has_bf16: bool = sysctl("hw.optional.arm.FEAT_BF16"); // M1 = false, future = maybe

// Derive tile size
let l2_per_core = l2 / cpus_per_l2 as usize;
let kc = (l2_per_core / 2) / (4 * MAX_N_TILE); // half L2 for weight tile
```

**Bandwidth cannot be queried — it must be measured.** The EP should run a one-shot streaming-read benchmark at first use (takes <100 ms with a 64 MB buffer, 3 iterations). Cache the result to avoid re-measuring every session.

```rust
fn measure_cpu_read_bandwidth(p_cores: u32) -> f64 {
    // Spawn p_cores threads, each reads 64 MiB sequentially, 3 reps
    // Return aggregate GB/s (best of 3)
    // QOS_CLASS_USER_INTERACTIVE to target P-cores
}
```

**SLC size is not exposed by sysctl.** Do not depend on it for correctness. If needed for optional tuning, probe it with the cache-level bandwidth benchmark (inflection point between L2-speed and DRAM-speed reads).

---

## 8. Benchmark Methodology (for Pris)

### Authoritative metrics
1. **Decode tok/s** (absolute) — the user-facing number
2. **Decode BW utilization** = `(tok/s × W_bytes) / BW_cpu_measured` — the roofline fraction

Both must be reported. A run on an M1 Air showing 14 tok/s at 90% utilization is excellent; the same 14 tok/s on an M1 Max at 14% utilization is a regression.

### Protocol
1. **Warmup:** 1 full generation pass (discarded)
2. **Iterations:** minimum 5, report median and p95
3. **Token count:** generate 50 tokens (discard first 2 for tok/s)
4. **Prompt:** fixed 40-token prompt
5. **Thermal:** 30s cooldown between runs; discard if frequency drops >10%
6. **Machine idle:** <5% background CPU (`top -l 1`)
7. **Statistical significance:** change is real if median ≥5% AND p95 ≥3% across 5 runs

### Regression floor
**Do NOT use a bare global tok/s constant.** Instead:
- On the measurement rig (M1 Max, detected by `hw.perflevel0.physicalcpu == 8` + `hw.memsize == 32 GiB`): assert `decode_toks >= FLOOR_TOKS` (absolute)
- On any other Apple Silicon: assert `bw_utilization >= 0.30` (30% of measured ceiling)
- On non-Apple-Silicon: skip gracefully

### Commands
```bash
# ORT baseline
cargo run --release -p onnx-genai -- profile models/qwen2.5-0.5b --execution-provider cpu --num-tokens 50

# Native
cargo run --release -p onnx-genai -- profile models/qwen2.5-0.5b --execution-provider cpu --native --num-tokens 50
```

---

## 9. Summary

| Metric | This M1 Max (measured) | Formula (any Apple Silicon) |
|--------|----------------------|---------------------------|
| BW ceiling | 197 GB/s (8P) | Measure at startup |
| Weight size | 1.932 GB (FP32) | From model file |
| AI (decode) | 0.50 FLOP/byte | 2 / bytes_per_param |
| Decode ceiling | 102 tok/s | BW_cpu / W_bytes |
| ORT | 45.45 tok/s (44.6%) | ~45% of ceiling (expected similar) |
| Native | 3.48 tok/s (2.9%) | Pathological everywhere (same bug) |
| Root cause | Single-threaded `gemm_generic` | Universal — same code path on all Macs |
| Fix (universal) | Wire Accelerate + NEON MT GEMV | Scales with runtime-detected topology |
| Beat-ORT ceiling | 58–67 tok/s on this machine | `0.55–0.65 × BW_cpu / W_bytes` |

---

## ADDENDUM: Fix B De-Risk — Accelerate GEMV Empirical Verdict (2026-07-27)

**Date:** 2026-07-27T04:33Z
**Purpose:** Empirically determine whether Fix B (Accelerate BLAS) alone clears ORT's 45.45 tok/s, or whether Fix C (NEON MT GEMV) is required.

### Exact Model Shapes (from ONNX graph initializers)

All weights stored transposed as `weight_t [K, N]`:

| Projection | Shape [K, N] | Size (FP32) | × Layers | Total |
|-----------|-------------|-------------|----------|-------|
| q_proj | 896 × 896 | 3.2 MB | 24 | 76.8 MB |
| k_proj | 896 × 128 | 0.5 MB | 24 | 11.0 MB |
| v_proj | 896 × 128 | 0.5 MB | 24 | 11.0 MB |
| o_proj | 896 × 896 | 3.2 MB | 24 | 76.8 MB |
| gate_proj | 896 × 4864 | 17.4 MB | 24 | 418.0 MB |
| up_proj | 896 × 4864 | 17.4 MB | 24 | 418.0 MB |
| down_proj | 4864 × 896 | 17.4 MB | 24 | 418.0 MB |
| lm_head (tied embed) | 896 × 151936 | 544.5 MB | 1 | 544.5 MB |
| **Total** | | | | **1975.8 MB** |

### Q1: What GB/s does cblas_sgemv achieve at exact decode shapes?

| Shape | Matrix Size | cblas_sgemv (best) | cblas_sgemv (avg) | MT NEON 8T | Accelerate Deficit |
|-------|-----------|-------------------|------------------|-----------|-------------------|
| q_proj [896×896] | 3.2 MB (L2) | **137 GB/s** | 132 GB/s | 26 GB/s | NEON loses (thread overhead) |
| k_proj [896×128] | 0.5 MB (L2) | **151 GB/s** | 128 GB/s | 5 GB/s | NEON loses (thread overhead) |
| v_proj [896×128] | 0.5 MB (L2) | **143 GB/s** | 139 GB/s | 6 GB/s | NEON loses (thread overhead) |
| o_proj [896×896] | 3.2 MB (L2) | **156 GB/s** | 132 GB/s | 35 GB/s | NEON loses (thread overhead) |
| gate_proj [896×4864] | 17.4 MB (DRAM) | 59 GB/s | 47 GB/s | **114 GB/s** | Accelerate 2× slower |
| up_proj [896×4864] | 17.4 MB (DRAM) | 58 GB/s | 41 GB/s | **124 GB/s** | Accelerate 2× slower |
| down_proj [4864×896] | 17.4 MB (DRAM) | 59 GB/s | 49 GB/s | **112 GB/s** | Accelerate 2× slower |
| lm_head [896×151936] | 544.5 MB (DRAM) | 35 GB/s | 33 GB/s | **111 GB/s** | Accelerate **3× slower** |

**Two regimes are visible:**
- **L2-resident matrices (≤3.2 MB):** Accelerate wins at 137–156 GB/s. Its internal GCD threading is well-tuned for cache-resident GEMV. MT NEON with pthread_create/join loses badly due to thread-spawn overhead dominating ~3 µs of actual work.
- **DRAM-bound matrices (≥17.4 MB):** Accelerate collapses to 35–59 GB/s. MT NEON achieves 111–124 GB/s — **2–3× faster**. The FFN projections (gate/up/down) and LM head dominate decode cost (~91% of weight bytes), so this regime determines the outcome.

**Full decode step totals:**

| Approach | Time | tok/s | Eff. BW | vs ORT |
|----------|------|-------|---------|--------|
| **cblas_sgemv** | 38.4 ms | **26.1** | 51.5 GB/s | 0.57× ❌ |
| **MT NEON FP32 (8T, pthread)** | 25.1 ms | **39.8** | 78.7 GB/s | 0.88× ❌ |
| **MT NEON FP16→FP32 (8T, pthread)** | 21.8 ms | **45.8** | 45.3 GB/s | 1.01× ≈ |
| ORT | 22.0 ms | **45.45** | 87.8 GB/s | 1.00× |
| MT NEON FP32 (persistent pool, est.) | ~17 ms | **~59** | ~116 GB/s | 1.30× ✅ |

### Q2: Is cblas_sgemv internally multi-threaded?

**Partially, but inadequately for DRAM-bound shapes.**

Sustained 2-second runs:
- LM head (544 MB): 25.8 GB/s sustained — **below single-core bandwidth (50–60 GB/s)**. Accelerate's GEMV is effectively single-threaded or poorly threaded for very large matrices.
- gate_proj (17.4 MB): 35.0 GB/s sustained — also below single-core ceiling.
- Small matrices (3.2 MB): 137–156 GB/s — this IS multi-threaded, using GCD. The L2-cache-resident working set allows effective sharing.

**Diagnosis:** Accelerate's GEMV has a "small = multi-threaded, large = essentially single-threaded" pathology. For large DRAM-bound matrices, it achieves only 35–59 GB/s — roughly what a single core can sustain. This is structurally the same problem as `gemm_generic` parallelizing over M: the internal parallelism doesn't scale to the DRAM streaming regime.

### Q3: Does calling Accelerate from inside a Rayon parallel region oversubscribe?

**Yes — severely.**

8 concurrent Rayon workers each calling cblas_sgemv on gate_proj [896×4864]:

| Concurrent callers | Per-thread BW | Aggregate BW | Slowdown per thread |
|-------------------|--------------|-------------|-------------------|
| 1 | 38.9 GB/s | 38.9 GB/s | — |
| 2 | 28.7 GB/s | 55.7 GB/s | 0.74× |
| 4 | 18.3 GB/s | 65.7 GB/s | 0.47× |
| 8 | 9.7 GB/s | 71.7 GB/s | 0.25× |

Aggregate BW gains saturate at ~72 GB/s with 8 callers — less than a single well-written MT GEMV (112 GB/s). Per-thread throughput drops 4× due to GCD-vs-pthread thread contention.

**Guidance for Iran:** Do NOT call cblas_sgemv from multiple Rayon workers concurrently. Either:
- Call it from a single thread (the "Accelerate path"), or
- Replace it entirely with a custom row-parallel GEMV on the Rayon pool (Fix C).
Mixing both thread pools is the worst option.

### Q4: Is cblas_sgemm(M=1) better or worse than cblas_sgemv?

**Layout-dependent — no clear winner.**

| Shape | sgemm(M=1) | sgemv(Trans) | sgemv(NoTrans, transposed layout) |
|-------|-----------|-------------|----------------------------------|
| q_proj 896×896 | 161 GB/s | 161 GB/s | 134 GB/s |
| gate_proj 896×4864 | 34 GB/s | 38 GB/s | **53 GB/s** |
| down_proj 4864×896 | 56 GB/s | **65 GB/s** | 46 GB/s |
| lm_head 896×151936 | 33 GB/s | 33 GB/s | 34 GB/s |

For DRAM-bound shapes:
- sgemv(NoTrans) on a pre-transposed [N,K] matrix beats sgemm(M=1) by 1.5× for wide matrices (gate/up)
- sgemv(Trans) on the stored [K,N] beats for tall matrices (down)
- Neither route exceeds 65 GB/s — both far below MT NEON's 112 GB/s

**Iran should not spend time optimizing Accelerate call conventions.** The ceiling is structurally too low.

### Q5: Do concurrent independent GEMVs beat one big threaded GEMV?

**No — for cache-resident shapes (q/k/v projections).**

q+k+v sequential: 29.5 µs → 140 GB/s
q+k+v concurrent (3 threads): 28.0 µs → 147 GB/s (1.05× speedup)

Accelerate already multi-threads the individual small-matrix GEMVs internally, so launching them concurrently adds nothing. For DRAM-bound shapes, concurrent calls cause oversubscription (see Q3).

### FP16 / Half-Precision Analysis

**FP16 weights halve the bytes per token and nearly double the ceiling.**

Measured (8 threads, NEON, pthread_create/join):

| Approach | Full decode time | tok/s | Weight bytes/tok |
|----------|-----------------|-------|-----------------|
| FP32 MT NEON | 25.1 ms | 39.8 | 1976 MB |
| FP16→FP32 widen GEMV | 21.8 ms | **45.8** | 988 MB |
| Native FP16 FMLA (big shapes only) | — | — | 988 MB |

Native FP16 FMLA results on dominant shapes:

| Shape | FP16 size | FP16 FMLA (8T) | Equivalent FP32 BW |
|-------|----------|---------------|-------------------|
| gate_proj | 8.7 MB | 64 GB/s | 128 GB/s |
| up_proj | 8.7 MB | 74 GB/s | 149 GB/s |
| down_proj | 8.7 MB | 81 GB/s | 162 GB/s |
| lm_head | 272 MB | **94 GB/s** | **188 GB/s** |

The LM head at 94 GB/s FP16 = 188 GB/s FP32-equivalent — **near the 197 GB/s raw ceiling!** FP16 saturates the memory bus.

**Ceiling projections:**

| Config | This M1 Max | M1 Air (est 35 GB/s) |
|--------|------------|---------------------|
| FP32, MT NEON | 59 tok/s (persistent pool est.) | ~15 tok/s |
| FP16, MT NEON | **~100 tok/s** | **~35 tok/s** |

**Runtime detection (universal across Apple Silicon):**
- `hw.optional.arm.FEAT_FP16 == 1` — true on ALL Apple Silicon. NEON FP16 load/store/convert is universal.
- `vfmaq_f16` (native FP16 FMLA) requires ARMv8.2 `fullfp16` — all Apple Silicon supports this.
- `hw.optional.arm.FEAT_BF16` — false on M1/M2, true on M3+. Not needed for this path.
- Accelerate has NO `cblas_hgemv` or `cblas_hgemm`. FP16 GEMV must be custom NEON.
- **Tier-dependent:** The FP16 bandwidth advantage is proportionally similar across chips (halving bytes helps equally). The absolute tok/s ceiling scales with each chip's measured BW.

**Accuracy note:** FP16 weights with FP32 accumulation is standard practice (matches PyTorch AMP). Pure FP16 accumulation (native FMLA) is faster but may have precision issues for long K-reductions (K=4864). The widen-to-FP32 path is the safe default; native FP16 is an optional acceleration when accuracy is verified.

---

## VERDICT FOR IRAN

### Fix B (Accelerate BLAS) alone: ❌ Does NOT beat ORT.

cblas_sgemv achieves **26.1 tok/s** (51.5 GB/s effective) — a 7.5× improvement over native's 3.48 tok/s, but **42% slower than ORT's 45.45 tok/s**. The reason is structural: Accelerate's GEMV is poorly multi-threaded for DRAM-bound matrices (35–59 GB/s on the shapes that matter), while ORT's MLAS achieves 88 GB/s through its own persistent thread pool and optimized NEON kernels.

### Fix C (NEON MT GEMV) is REQUIRED: ✅

A row-parallel NEON GEMV on the existing Rayon/SPMD pool achieves 111–124 GB/s on the dominant shapes. With persistent threads (no pthread_create/join), estimated full-decode: **~59 tok/s (FP32), ~100 tok/s (FP16)**.

### Recommended Architecture for Iran

**Hybrid approach — best of both worlds:**

1. **Small, L2-resident matrices (≤ ~4 MB):** call `cblas_sgemv` from a single thread. Accelerate excels here at 137–156 GB/s with zero setup cost. These are q/k/v/o projections (3.2 MB or smaller). Do NOT parallelize these with the Rayon pool — the thread overhead exceeds the work.

2. **Large, DRAM-bound matrices (> ~4 MB):** use custom row-parallel NEON GEMV on the existing persistent SPMD/Rayon pool. These are gate/up/down projections (17.4 MB each) and the LM head (544 MB). This is where all the time goes and where MT NEON has a 2–3× advantage over Accelerate.

3. **Threshold:** `if weight_bytes < l2_per_core { accelerate } else { mt_neon }` — query `hw.perflevel0.l2cachesize / hw.perflevel0.cpusperl2` at startup.

4. **Never call Accelerate from inside a Rayon parallel region.** The oversubscription destroys throughput.

5. **FP16 weights are the clear next step** after Fix C lands. Halving bytes nearly doubles the ceiling. NEON FP16 is universal on Apple Silicon. No Accelerate dependency.

### What Is Universal vs Tier-Dependent

| Finding | Universal? |
|---------|-----------|
| cblas_sgemv is single-threaded for large matrices | 🟢 Universal (all macOS Accelerate) |
| MT NEON GEMV beats Accelerate for DRAM-bound shapes | 🟢 Universal (NEON is ARMv8 baseline) |
| Optimal small/large threshold depends on L2 size | 🟡 Tier-dep (query L2 at runtime) |
| FP16 FMLA throughput | 🟢 Universal (FEAT_FP16 on all Apple Silicon) |
| Absolute tok/s ceilings | 🟡 Tier-dep (scales with measured BW) |
| Oversubscription with Accelerate + Rayon | 🟢 Universal (GCD contention is structural) |

---

## ⚠️ CORRECTION: 197 GB/s Bandwidth Ceiling Was Wrong — Superseded (2026-07-27)

**The 197 GB/s 8-core streaming figure reported in Section 2 is not reproducible and is hereby superseded.** The authoritative ceiling is **112 GB/s**, independently confirmed by Fact Checker (119.7 GB/s peak across three methods), Pris's harness probe (108.3 GB/s), and my own MT GEMV measurements (108–112 GB/s across all sessions).

### Root Cause of the Error

I reproduced the original `bw_bench` and diagnosed three compounding factors:

**Factor 1: "Sum-of-per-thread-best" metric (1.5× inflation)**

The original benchmark returned the sum of each thread's individually-best iteration across 5 independent runs. These peaks do not occur simultaneously — each thread's fastest iteration likely corresponds to different wall-clock windows (one thread gets a clean cache line while another is stalled). Measured directly:

```
8 threads: sum-of-best = 126.9 GB/s, wall-clock aggregate = 85.0 GB/s → 1.5× inflation
```

This metric is fundamentally broken for concurrent bandwidth measurement. The correct metric is total bytes moved / wall-clock time across all threads.

**Factor 2: Compiler load elision (~1.18× inflation)**

The original loop used `volatile unsigned long sink` but only assigned it once after the loop. The compiler may reorder, merge, or speculate loads within the non-volatile accumulation loop. Adding an `asm volatile("" : "+r"(acc))` barrier reduced the reported 8-thread number from 193 GB/s to 164 GB/s — an 18% drop, indicating the optimizer was eliding or reordering some fraction of the loads.

**Factor 3: Access-pattern mismatch (~2× for the workload that matters)**

The original benchmark streamed sequentially with pure adds — a pattern hardware prefetchers handle optimally by running far ahead of consumption. A real GEMV has:
- A multiply-add dependency chain per loaded weight
- A second memory stream (the input activation vector)
- Non-trivial instruction-level parallelism constraints

Measured: GEMV-like access achieved 89.5 GB/s (8 threads) vs 193 GB/s for pure streaming — **2.2× less**. Single-thread: 12.8 GB/s GEMV-like vs 61.5 GB/s streaming — 4.8× less.

**Combined effect:** `112 × 1.5 × 1.18 ≈ 198 GB/s` — almost exactly the reported 197 GB/s. All three factors compound.

### Corrected Ceilings (authoritative, supersedes all earlier numbers)

| Metric | Wrong (original) | Correct |
|--------|-----------------|---------|
| Achievable CPU BW (8 P-cores, GEMV workload) | 197 GB/s | **112 GB/s** |
| FP32 decode ceiling (1932 MB model) | 102 tok/s | **56.7 tok/s** |
| ORT at 45.45 tok/s, fraction of ceiling | 44.6% | **80.1%** |
| Headroom above ORT (FP32) | ~55% | **~25%** |

### What This Changes

ORT is at **80% of the hardware ceiling**, not 45%. The FP32 headroom above ORT is real but modest (~25%, not ~55%). A well-optimized FP32 GEMV with persistent thread pool can achieve 52–56 tok/s — a meaningful 15–25% win over ORT's 45.45, but not the 30%+ implied by the original analysis.

**The real headroom is FP16 and quantization**, exactly as the later analysis suggested. The original recommendation ranking was correct in direction — the magnitude was wrong.

### Lesson

**Two independent measurements that agree beat one confident outlier.** A roofline must use the achievable peak for the *relevant access pattern* (GEMV), not a pure-streaming benchmark's peak. The streaming benchmark measured the memory bus ceiling; the GEMV measures what the CPU can actually consume through a compute-bound pipeline. They are different numbers and the GEMV number is the one that matters for this workload.

---

## ADDENDUM: FP16 Feasibility — Measured and Corroborated (2026-07-27)

All measurements use two independent methods: (A) median of 7 single-pass wall clocks, (B) median of 5 batched-10 wall clocks. Only numbers where A and B agree within 15% are reported. This is the standing methodology going forward.

### Q1: Does achievable bandwidth change with FP16?

**The memory bus delivers the same ~110 GB/s regardless of data type.** FP16 wins by reading half the bytes at the same rate, not by achieving higher bandwidth.

Measured at real Qwen2.5-0.5B decode shapes, 8 P-core threads (methods A / B):

| Shape | FP32 GB/s | FP16→FP32 GB/s (FP16 bytes) | Native FP16 GB/s (FP16 bytes) |
|-------|----------|---------------------------|------------------------------|
| gate_proj (8.7/17.4 MB) | 109 / 126 | 40 / 48 | 59 / 58 |
| up_proj (8.7/17.4 MB) | 110 / 118 | 44 / 54 | 67 / 60 |
| down_proj (8.7/17.4 MB) | 111 / 111 | 55 / 54 | 72 / 75 |
| lm_head (272/544 MB) | 104 / 95 | 99 / 93 | 96 / 99 |

**Key finding:** The FP16→FP32 widening path has a **compute overhead** from `vcvt_f32_f16` instructions that limits throughput on medium matrices (gate/up/down at 8.7 MB). Only the LM head (272 MB, pure DRAM streaming) achieves near-parity with FP32's bandwidth. The native FP16 FMLA path avoids this overhead and achieves higher throughput.

Full decode step totals (corroborated, method A):

| Method | Decode ms | tok/s | GB/s eff. | vs ORT | % of corrected roof |
|--------|----------|-------|----------|--------|-------------------|
| FP32 NEON (8T, pthread) | 29.6 | **33.8** | 66.8 | 0.74× | 60% |
| FP16→FP32 NEON (8T, pthread) | 27.0 | **37.1** | 36.6 (FP16) | 0.82× | — |
| Native FP16 NEON (8T, pthread) | 21.6 | **46.3** | 45.8 (FP16) | **1.02×** | — |
| ORT (profile) | 22.0 | **45.45** | 87.8 | 1.00× | 78% |

**With persistent thread pool (estimated ceiling, no pthread overhead):**

| Method | Weight bytes | Ceiling GB/s | Ceiling tok/s |
|--------|-------------|-------------|--------------|
| FP32 | 1976 MB | 112 | 56.7 |
| FP16 native | 988 MB | ~96 (measured on LM head) | ~97 |

FP16 with a persistent pool projects to **~97 tok/s** — 2.1× ORT and 1.7× the FP32 ceiling.

### Q2: Is NEON FP16 arithmetic native on M1?

**Yes — it is native, not emulated.** Two distinct capabilities, both present on all Apple Silicon:

1. **FP16 storage + FP32 conversion** (`vcvt_f32_f16`, `vcvt_f16_f32`): ARMv8.0 baseline. Available everywhere. This loads FP16 data, widens to FP32, does FP32 FMA, then optionally narrows back. The conversion instructions add pipeline overhead.

2. **FP16 native arithmetic** (`vfmaq_f16`, FEAT_FP16): ARMv8.2. Requires `+fullfp16` target feature. Confirmed on this M1 Max via `sysctl hw.optional.arm.FEAT_FP16 = 1`. All Apple Silicon (M1–M4) has FEAT_FP16. This performs FP16 FMA directly in half-precision registers — no widening, 2× the elements per NEON register (8 vs 4).

**FP16 is both a bandwidth win AND a compute win on Apple Silicon.** With native FP16 FMLA:
- 8 elements per `float16x8_t` vs 4 per `float32x4_t` → 2× throughput per instruction
- 2 bytes per weight vs 4 → 2× less memory traffic
- The compute advantage is why native FP16 (46.3 tok/s) beats FP16→FP32 (37.1 tok/s) despite reading the same bytes

**Runtime detection:** `sysctl hw.optional.arm.FEAT_FP16` or `std::arch::is_aarch64_feature_detected!("fp16")` in Rust (unstable). On Apple Silicon this is always true, but the check is needed for portability to other ARM targets (e.g., Raspberry Pi 4 does NOT have FEAT_FP16).

### Q3: Accuracy cost

Measured over 1000 random dot products with realistic weight distribution (U(-0.05, 0.05)) and activation distribution (U(-1, 1)):

| Method | K | Typical rel error | Max rel error | Notes |
|--------|---|------------------|--------------|-------|
| FP32 NEON | 4864 | 3.9e-7 | 1.9e-3 | FP32 baseline noise |
| FP16 stor + FP32 acc | 4864 | 2.3e-4 | 3.8e+0 | Max from near-zero reference |
| Native FP16 acc | 4864 | 2.3e-3 | 1.1e+1 | Catastrophic on near-zero |
| FP16 stor + FP32 acc | 896 | 7.7e-6 | (lower) | Shorter K = less error |

**Interpretation for Chew:**

- The large max relative errors (3.8, 10.8) are **artifacts of near-zero reference values** (catastrophic cancellation where the true sum is ~0 and FP16 quantization pushes it slightly positive or negative). The absolute error in these cases is small (~1e-3). For LLM inference, near-zero logits correspond to very low-probability tokens and do not affect generation quality.

- **FP16 storage + FP32 accumulation** (typical error 2.3e-4): this matches the quality of PyTorch AMP and is standard practice for LLM inference. The ~10 bits of mantissa in FP16 weights + 23 bits in FP32 accumulators provide sufficient precision for all practical model sizes. **Recommended as the safe default.**

- **Native FP16 accumulation** (typical error 2.3e-3): 10× worse than FP32-accum, and the error grows with K. For K=4864 (FFN projections), accumulating 4864 terms in FP16 (~10 bits mantissa) risks ~6 bits of precision loss from rounding. For K=896 (attention projections), it's acceptable. **Use cautiously; verify per-model with top-1 agreement tests.**

- **BF16 is NOT available on M1/M2.** `sysctl hw.optional.arm.FEAT_BF16 = 0`. BF16 FMLA requires M3+ (ARMv8.6+). On M1/M2, BF16 would require scalar conversion → no bandwidth advantage over FP32. FP16 is the correct half-precision path for the M1/M2 family. On M3+, BF16 is also viable and has wider dynamic range (8-bit exponent vs FP16's 5-bit) at the cost of lower mantissa precision (7 bits vs 10 bits).

### Q4: INT8 / INT4 quantization — wired status on aarch64

**Examined:** `matmul_nbits.rs` and `block_quantized_matmul.rs`

| Kernel | aarch64 Status | Detail |
|--------|---------------|--------|
| **MatMulNBits INT4** (ONNX) | 🟡 Wired but suboptimal | `DotKernel::Neon` selected. But `uses_vnni_int4_direct()` returns `false` on aarch64 → takes the int8 dequant route, not direct int4 streaming. Functional but slower than optimal. |
| **MatMulNBits INT8** (ONNX) | 🟢 Wired with NEON | Uses `vmlal` (widen multiply-accumulate) baseline, bit-exact vs scalar. |
| **BlockQuantizedMatMul** (GGUF) | 🔴 Not wired | Zero aarch64/NEON references. Falls to scalar dequant + `gemm_generic`. Same unwired-placeholder pattern as `CpuBackend::Accelerate`. |
| **MLAS SQNBit** | ⬜ Not compiled | Behind `#[cfg(feature = "mlas")]`, which is x86-only opt-in. |

**INT4 ceiling projection (if properly wired):**
- FP32 weights: 1976 MB → INT4: ~247 MB (8× compression)
- At 112 GB/s: 247/112 = 2.2 ms → **~450 tok/s** (if compute doesn't bottleneck)
- INT4 dequant adds compute: AI increases to ~4 FLOP/byte, still bandwidth-bound on M1 Max
- On M1 Air (35 GB/s): 247/35 = 7.1 ms → ~141 tok/s — transformative even on low-BW parts

### Q5: Apple Silicon generality

| Feature | M1 | M2 | M3 | M4 | Runtime detection |
|---------|----|----|----|----|-------------------|
| NEON FP32 FMA | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Always (ARMv8 baseline) |
| FEAT_FP16 (native FP16 FMLA) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | `hw.optional.arm.FEAT_FP16` |
| FEAT_BF16 | ❌ | ❌ | ✅ | ✅ | `hw.optional.arm.FEAT_BF16` |
| FEAT_I8MM (int8 matrix multiply) | ❌ | ❌ | ✅? | ✅ | `hw.optional.arm.FEAT_I8MM` |
| SME (Scalable Matrix Extension) | ❌ | ❌ | ❌ | ✅ | Not in public sysctl; Accelerate routes to it |
| Accelerate BLAS (AMX-backed) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | Always on macOS |

**Conclusions that hold family-wide:**
- 🟢 FP16 storage + FP32 accumulation GEMV: works everywhere (ARMv8 baseline conversion)
- 🟢 Native FP16 FMLA GEMV: works on all Apple Silicon (all have FEAT_FP16)
- 🟢 Row-parallel NEON GEMV on Rayon pool: works everywhere (thread count from runtime)
- 🟢 Accelerate BLAS for small/cache-resident matrices: works everywhere
- 🟡 BF16: M3+ only — not a universal path, requires runtime check + FP16 fallback
- 🟡 I8MM / SDOT: varies by generation — existing NEON `vmlal` baseline is the safe path

---

## 7. FP16 End-to-End Baseline Pair (2026-07-27)

**Purpose:** Establish like-for-like FP16 numbers using the committed harness, before investing in an FP16 kernel. Justin's binding fairness rule: any "native beats ORT" claim must compare the same model and dtype.

**Method:** All measurements via Pris's `compare` harness (`cargo run --release -p onnx-genai-bench --features bench-native --bin compare`). Corroborated with `profile_native` on a different prompt. Memory (RSS) verified via `ps -o rss` during decode.

**Commands run:**
```bash
# FP32 baseline
cargo run --release -p onnx-genai-bench --features bench-native --bin compare -- \
  --model models/qwen2.5-0.5b --tokens 50 --decode-skip 2 --warmups 1 --runs 5 \
  --profile-json target/sebastian-fp32-compare.json

# FP16
cargo run --release -p onnx-genai-bench --features bench-native --bin compare -- \
  --model models/qwen2.5-0.5b-f16 --tokens 50 --decode-skip 2 --warmups 1 --runs 5 \
  --profile-json target/sebastian-f16-compare.json

# BF16 (native only — ORT fails with MatMul(13) unsupported)
cargo run --release -p onnx-genai-bench --features bench-native --bin compare -- \
  --model models/qwen2.5-0.5b-bf16 --direct-backend native --tokens 50 --decode-skip 2 --warmups 1 --runs 5

# Q4 ORT only (native fails: graph missing position_ids for q4-epcpu; q4-onnx uses ORT-specific Attention op)
cargo run --release -p onnx-genai-bench --features bench-native --bin compare -- \
  --model models/qwen2.5-0.5b-q4-onnx --direct-backend ort --tokens 50 --decode-skip 2 --warmups 1 --runs 5

# Q4 native (q4-onnx model)
cargo run --release -p onnx-genai-bench --features bench-native --bin compare -- \
  --model models/qwen2.5-0.5b-q4-onnx --direct-backend native --tokens 50 --decode-skip 2 --warmups 1 --runs 3
```

### 7.1 The Decisive Finding: Both Backends Widen FP16 → FP32

**Neither ORT nor native has a native FP16 compute kernel on CPU.** Both widen FP16 weights to FP32 before computation.

Evidence:
1. **FP16 is SLOWER than FP32 for both backends.** ORT: 42.21 vs 45.77 tok/s. Native: 26.06 vs 31.19 tok/s.
2. **RSS confirms double-buffering.** FP16 model resident memory ≈ 5.0 GB for both backends, vs ~3.0 GB for FP32. The extra ~2 GB is the widened FP32 copy of the 994 MB FP16 weights.
3. **Native TTFT doubles.** FP16 native TTFT = 2046 ms vs FP32 = 1026 ms. The extra 1020 ms is spent in `to_dense_f32_widen()` (line 580, dtype.rs) doing scalar per-element `.to_f32()` conversion with allocation. On aarch64, there is no NEON widen path (the F16C path is x86-only, line 594-608). The MatMulPrepack caches the widened FP32 buffer for constant (model weight) inputs, so subsequent decodes use cached FP32 data.
4. **The weight data streamed per token is 1985 MB, not 994 MB.** The FP16 model file saves disk space but provides zero bandwidth benefit at decode time.

### 7.2 Full Results Table

Authoritative ceiling: 112 GB/s achievable bandwidth (GEMV workload, this M1 Max).

| Model | Backend | Decode tok/s | TTFT ms | Model Load ms | Actual MB/tok | Eff. GB/s | Roof % | Notes |
|-------|---------|-------------|---------|--------------|---------------|-----------|--------|-------|
| FP32 (1985 MB) | ORT | **45.77** | 102.7 | 1256.7 | 1985 | 90.9 | **81.1%** | Baseline reference |
| FP32 (1985 MB) | Native | 31.19 | 1025.5 | 107.7 | 1985 | 61.9 | 55.3% | No persistent pool active |
| FP16 (994 MB) | ORT | **42.21** | 107.4 | 1641.6 | 1985† | 83.8 | **74.8%** | †Widens to FP32, RSS=5.0 GB |
| FP16 (994 MB) | Native | 26.06 | 2046.1 | 112.4 | 1985† | 51.7 | 46.2% | †Scalar widen, RSS=5.0 GB |
| BF16 (994 MB) | ORT | ❌ | — | — | — | — | — | ORT MatMul(13) unsupported |
| BF16 (994 MB) | Native | 6.02 | 2051.1 | 107.8 | ? | ? | ? | Severe fallback, ~5× slower than FP32 |
| Q4 (1325 MB) | ORT | **44.37** | 106.7 | 462.6 | ? | ? | ? | MatMulNBits, same speed as FP32 |
| Q4 (1325 MB) | Native | 12.41 | 1269.3 | 109.5 | ? | ? | ? | Suboptimal int8 dequant route |

**Corroboration (standing rule):** Each FP16 number confirmed via two independent methods:
- `compare` harness: 5 runs, median with p10-p95 spread
- `profile_native`: 3 runs with warmup, different prompt
- ORT FP16 corroboration: 42.21 (run 1) → 41.31 (run 2, different prompt). ✓ consistent.
- Native FP16 corroboration: 26.06 (compare) → 24.80 (profile_native avg). ✓ consistent (profile_native includes TTFT).

### 7.3 Coherence Verification

All backends produce identical, coherent text across FP32 and FP16:
```
"The function should take a single string argument and return a new string
 that is the reverse of the input string. The function should not use any
 built-in string reversal functions or methods..."
```
Token IDs match across FP32 and FP16 for both ORT and native (greedy decoding, temperature=0). FP16 → FP32 widening preserves numerics.

### 7.4 The FP16 Opportunity — Verdict

**The FP16 phase is STRONGLY worth doing.** The opportunity is BIGGER than pre-measurement expectations.

Justin's concern: "if ORT also gets substantially faster on FP16, the FP16 win over ORT may be much smaller." The answer: **ORT gets 8% SLOWER on FP16** (42.21 vs 45.77 tok/s) because it also widens to FP32 on CPU. ORT has no native FP16 kernel on the CPU execution provider.

Projected outcome with a proper native FP16 NEON GEMV kernel (streaming 994 MB of FP16 weights, FP32 accumulate):

| Scenario | Native FP16 tok/s | vs ORT FP16 (42.21) | vs ORT FP32 (45.77) |
|----------|-------------------|---------------------|---------------------|
| 100% roof (theoretical max) | 112.7 | 2.67× | 2.46× |
| 80% roof (ORT-level efficiency) | 90.1 | 2.14× | 1.97× |
| 55% roof (current native efficiency) | 62.0 | 1.47× | 1.35× |

Even at the current native efficiency level (55% roof — no persistent pool, imperfect parallelism), a proper FP16 kernel would yield 62 tok/s, beating ORT FP32 by 35%.

**The fair like-for-like comparison (same FP16 model):**
- ORT FP16: 42.21 tok/s (widens to FP32, wasteful)
- Native FP16 projected: 62–90 tok/s (reads half the bytes)
- Win: 1.5–2.1×

**Why this works:** ORT's FP16 CPU path is fundamentally unable to benefit from reduced precision — it widens to FP32 before the GEMM kernel. A native FP16 NEON GEMV kernel reads FP16 directly from memory, computes with 8 elements per `vfmaq_f16` instruction (vs 4 for FP32), and achieves both a bandwidth win AND a compute throughput win.

### 7.5 What Must Be Built for the FP16 Win

1. **NEON FP16 GEMV kernel** in `matmul.rs` — reads FP16 weights, FP32 input/output, `vfmlalq_low_f16` or `vfmaq_f16` with FP32 accumulator
2. **Skip `to_dense_f32_widen`** for FP16 weight tensors — the kernel must read FP16 directly from the mmap'd buffer, not from a widened copy
3. **Persistent thread pool** — Iran's work on the FP32 path (persistent SPMD pool) is prerequisite; FP16 kernel plugs into the same pool
4. **Runtime detection:** `is_aarch64_feature_detected!("fp16")` (ARMv8.2, all Apple Silicon, but not all ARM CPUs)

### 7.6 BF16 and Q4 Assessment

**BF16:**
- ORT cannot load BF16 models (MatMul(13) unsupported on CPU EP)
- Native BF16 achieves only 6.02 tok/s — 5× slower than FP32. Root cause not fully diagnosed but likely involves: (a) bf16 GEMM fast path requires avx512_bf16 (x86 only), (b) on aarch64, every decode step may be re-widening rather than using cached FP32, or (c) a different code path entirely
- BF16 arithmetic (FEAT_BF16) is M3+ only — not universal. Not recommended as a priority path.
- **Verdict:** BF16 is not viable as a near-term lever. FP16 is strictly better: same 2-byte storage, native arithmetic support on all Apple Silicon, and no ORT incompatibility.

**Q4 (INT4 with MatMulNBits):**
- ORT Q4: 44.37 tok/s — **nearly identical to FP32** (45.77). The dequantization overhead on aarch64 negates the reduced bytes. ORT's MatMulNBits on ARM is not well-optimized.
- Native Q4: 12.41 tok/s — `uses_vnni_int4_direct()` returns false on aarch64, so int4 goes through suboptimal int8 dequant route.
- The q4-epcpu model (designed for native EP) has incompatible graph structure (missing position_ids input). The q4-onnx model uses ORT-specific `Attention` op that native can't execute.
- **Verdict:** Q4 is the long-term ceiling (~450 tok/s theoretical) but requires both a well-optimized int4 kernel AND a compatible model export. Not actionable this sprint.

### 7.7 Apple Silicon Generality

| Finding | Universal? | Notes |
|---------|-----------|-------|
| Both backends widen FP16→FP32 | 🟢 Universal | Code path is arch-independent |
| FP16 slower than FP32 (both backends) | 🟢 Universal | Widening overhead exists everywhere |
| `to_dense_f32_widen` has no aarch64 NEON path | 🟢 Universal | Missing for all ARM, not just Apple |
| FP16 NEON FMLA (FEAT_FP16) available | 🟢 All Apple Silicon | ARMv8.2 baseline. Verify with `is_aarch64_feature_detected!("fp16")` on non-Apple ARM. |
| Achievable bandwidth varies 1.5–10× | 🟡 Tier-dependent | M1 Air ~68 GB/s → M4 Ultra ~800 GB/s. Ceiling formula scales linearly. |
| FP16 win ratio is bandwidth-independent | 🟢 Universal | 2× byte reduction → 2× ceiling at any BW tier. Actual win depends on kernel efficiency, which should be similar across chips. |
| BF16 arithmetic | 🔴 M3+ only | `is_aarch64_feature_detected!("bf16")`. M1/M2 must use FP16 or FP32. |
